### Page 20

need to replace them: a 20% productivity gain across 15 million
employees would deliver the same value. Consumers alone won't
close the gap; the money has to come from enterprises.
Two caveats. Concentration: the weight of the largest tech stocks in
the S&P 500 exceeds the dotcom peak, so any disappointment
would hit the whole index hard. And circularity in ﬁnancing and
revenues makes true end-demand harder to measure.
Finally, a bubble in prices doesn't mean the technology is a dud.

Railways had a bubble. So did the internet. Both still changed the
world.
What could end the boom prematurely:
disappointing demand, power bottlenecks, a
cheaper-model breakthrough, or Taiwan?
Most likely not a single trigger, but a slow squeeze: overcapacity,
eroding margins and tighter ﬁnancing if the revenue gap fails to
close.
Start with the usual suspects. Demand could disappoint if
enterprise adoption stalls and pilots fail to turn into proﬁts. Power

bottlenecks are more likely to cap the pace of the boom than to end
it, and could even stretch it out. A Taiwan crisis remains a low-
probability, extreme-impact event.
Cheaper models are the most intriguing risk. The DeepSeek shock of
January 2025 wiped almost US$600bn oﬀ Nvidia's market value in
a single day. Cheaper compute can raise demand – the Jevons
paradox – but higher volumes can come with lower margins. That
makes the proﬁtability of AI labs such as OpenAI and Anthropic the

big question mark. If it disappoints, the hardware will still be
needed, but the build-out would likely slow. Nvidia's exceptionally
high margins could also normalise if competition increases or the
scarcity value of leading-edge compute declines.
Missing from most lists is ﬁnancing: rising bond yields, a credit
event at a leveraged neocloud or private credit fund, or an energy
shock that keeps central banks hawkish.

### Page 21

Ultimately, we see the biggest risk in overcapacity. Continued
innovation in chips, more eﬃcient models and optimised
algorithms could eventually create an imbalance between supply
and demand, pushing down utilisation and returns. For now,
though, the industry is still struggling to scale compute and secure
enough power, while new business models and applications keep
emerging.
There is one more way to read today's capacity commitments: as
strategic entry deterrence. Announcing gigantic investment plans

discourages competitors from entering the market – and some of
those plans may never be fully executed if returns disappoint. That
would be a quieter ending than a crash. Not with a bang, but with a
series of downward revisions to capex guidance.
If tech stocks fall 30%, how much does the
wealth effect hit US consumption?
Less than you might think, but more than during the dotcom bust –
and the real damage would come through investment and jobs.
US equity wealth is highly concentrated. The top 20% of

households by income (earning more than $155k a year) hold
84.1% of equities, mutual funds and deﬁned-contribution pension
assets. That limits the wealth eﬀect. Fed research suggests the
marginal propensity to consume out of wealth fell from around 3.5
cents per dollar (1964-2011) to 2.6 cents in 2020.
A 30% stock market correction would wipe out roughly $24.5tn of
household wealth. At 2.6 cents per dollar, that means a drop in
consumer spending of around $640bn, or 2.9% of nominal

consumer spending (an annualised $22.3tn in August). With wealth
even more concentrated than in 2020, the multiplier is probably
weaker; at 2.2 cents, the hit would be around 2.4%. That is a level
eﬀect, typically playing out over one to two years – but a big
number nonetheless.
History oﬀers some comfort: during the dotcom bust, US consumer
spending barely ﬂinched. But it could be worse this time. First,
wealthy households are currently the only ones holding up

spending; the bottom 60%, who spend roughly as many dollars as
the top 20%, are squeezed by tariﬀs and higher fuel prices. Second,
the Fed has less room to cut than in 2001. Third, the credit cushion
is thinner: rising delinquencies on car loans and credit cards show
stress building at lower incomes.
The good news: this is not 2008. US household debt stands at
around 70% of GDP, the same as in 2001 and well below the 100%
reached in 2007.
In the end, the wealth eﬀect is the smaller channel. After the

dotcom bust, business investment fell by 11% in two years. IT
equipment was the least of it, falling just 6%, transport equipment
fell 21% and manufacturing buildings plunged by a whopping
55%. Tech may light the fuse, but the explosion happens elsewhere.
What would a tech sell-off mean for the
dollar and FX markets?
Not necessarily the classic safe-haven dollar rally – this time, a
tech sell-oﬀ could well hurt the dollar.
The usual playbook says risk-oﬀ means dollar up. But an AI-led

correction would be a US-centric shock. Foreign investors hold large,
partly unhedged positions in US equities, built up over a decade of
American outperformance. A sell-oﬀ in US tech could trigger
repatriation and higher hedge ratios, both dollar-negative – as in
the 'sell America' episode of April 2025. The IMF estimates the
dollar would fall by around 6% in an AI-led equity correction.
The rates channel points in the same direction. Negative wealth

eﬀects, weaker consumption and slower US growth would
eventually push the Fed towards rate cuts, narrowing interest rate
diﬀerentials against the dollar. The timing would depend on
inﬂation: with prices still running above target, the Fed may cut
later than markets hope, which could limit the dollar's fall in the
early stages.
Among the winners, the yen and the Swiss franc would play their
traditional safe-haven role. The euro could beneﬁt relatively, given

Europe's lower exposure to both AI capex and equity wealth (4Q). A

tech crash made in America would, for once, be a reason to hold

The losers would be the currencies of Asian tech exporters, notably
the Taiwan dollar and the Korean won, whose economies are
leveraged bets on AI demand. They would come under heavy
pressure, although intervention by local authorities and the legacy
of huge trade surpluses would cushion the blow somewhat.
The bottom line: in an AI bust, the dollar may lose its reputation as

the place to hide. Not because the world has found a better safe
haven, but because this time the ﬁre would start in the safe house.
Our base case for 2027: boom, plateau or
bust, and which signposts should readers
watch?
Our base case is a plateau at altitude: the boom continues in 2027,
but its contribution to growth peaks. A bust remains the main risk
scenario.
Early indicators point to tight semiconductor supply into 2028,
suggesting the investment cycle still has legs. But as capex growth

slows, AI's contribution to US growth should fade.
The diﬀerence between the two scenarios matters enormously for
the US. A plateau would give the non-tech economy room to
breathe: capital, energy and workers no longer absorbed by AI
could ﬂow back, allowing non-tech investment – and potentially
employment – to rebound. A bust would be a very diﬀerent animal:
an abrupt choking oﬀ of tech investment, similar to 2001,
combined with a negative wealth eﬀect on consumers through

falling stock markets. And this time, central banks would have less
room for rate cuts as inﬂation remains their main concern.
Which signposts should readers watch? For the boom itself, the key
indicators are hyperscaler capex guidance, Taiwanese order books
and lead times, and AI revenues relative to capex. Markets will focus
on realised AI revenues at the large technology ﬁrms, margins on
incremental revenue and how the next phase of the build-out will

economy, the questions are whether non-tech investment ﬁnally
recovers, how hiring in AI-exposed sectors develops, and what
productivity, grid connection and power data are telling us.
The question for 2027 isn't whether AI will change the world. It's
whether anyone gets paid for it in time. If earnings fail to match
sky-high expectations, the plateau could turn out to be a ledge.

## Document 314: Cliff Notes_ the source and persistence of inflation (2026-11-06)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Cliff Notes_ the source and persistence of inflation (2026-11-06).pdf

### Page 1

#cliffnotes #rba #australia #inflation #us #fomc #ecb #aud #usd
Cliff Notes: the source and persistence of inflation
By Ryan Wells, Illiana Jain & Elliot Clarke
11:16 October 30 2026 Share
Key insights from the week that was.
In Australia this week, all eyes were on the September CPI. Headline inflation rose 0.8% (4.3%yr) in
the month, largely owing to a 9.4% jump in fuel prices and a seasonal surge in domestic and
international holiday travel & accommodation. The underlying inflation pulse also proved hotter than

anticipated for Q3 overall, up 1.0% (3.7%yr).

### Page 2

While some parts of the basket are seeing disinflation – housing in particular looks to be cooling faster
than expected as builders lure sales with discounts – other components are still showing persistence.
Highlighting this is market services ex volatiles, which the RBA watches closely as an indicator of price
pressures related to domestic capacity. In Q3, this metric rose 1.2% (3.2%yr), and the monthly detail
points to momentum holding up – in line with our expectations for another chunky read in Q4.

With inflation running well above the RBA’s August forecasts, and some of the energy risks flagged
then materialising, we continue to expect the Monetary Policy Board will deliver a 25bp rate hike to
4.85% in November. Beyond November, another rate hike in February is a risk, but is not currently part
of our base case.
Across the Tasman, the RBNZ this week surprised market expectations for a 25bp rate hike, deciding
instead to wait for further information before taking the next step with policy. The decision was made

4:3, with the Governor in the ‘on hold’ camp. A 25bp hike in December to 3.00% and further tightening
in 2027 (Westpac NZ forecasts a peak of 4.00% in September) seems probable; however, each
decision will depend on fuel prices, inflation expectations and evidence of second-round pricing
pressures, along with the evolution of global economic risks.
In the US, the FOMC also held rates steady at its October meeting. Based on prior comments by key
members, the decision was not a surprise, and neither were Governor Cook and Cleveland Fed

President Hammack’s dissents in favour of a 25bp hike. The Committee remains positive on economic
activity, characterising it in the statement as “expanding at a strong pace” (an upgrade from “solid” in
September). Chair Warsh also subsequently emphasised the breadth of economic growth outside of
tech-related investment (evinced by Q3 GDP the day after; see below).
The Committee refrained from spotlighting key price risks in the statement. However, inflation “remains

elevated” and the FOMC are determined to achieve a “timely” and sustainable return of annual inflation
to 2.0%yr after 6 years above target. This guidance and the “accumulation of more information will help
the Committee determine the policy stance” suggests another 25bp hike is likely in December.
Thereafter, the FOMC will remain attentive to any broadening in inflation and related risks through
2027; although, in the absence of another shock, we expect them to remain on hold.

The Q3 GDP report certainly supported Chair Warsh’s view on the breadth of growth, the 3.4%
annualised gain for GDP benefitting from a 3.8% annualised increase in household consumption,
12.7% growth in non-residential investment as well as a robust increase in government spending.
Notably though, the contribution from business investment was effectively offset by net exports’ drag
given most of the componentry related to AI infrastructure investment is imported. This is not to say that

US firms won’t benefit from the investment once complete, but simply that much of the initial return
goes to the foreign businesses who provide the necessary capital equipment.
On consumption, the monthly PCE data implies downside risks remain for the consumer, the increase
in nominal spending seen in the month requiring dissaving and, on a real basis, consumption flat in the
month following a 0.5% gain in August. Cost-of-living pressures and uncertainty over the outlook is

### Page 3

Related articles
expected to see household consumption growth average around trend over the coming year, albeit with
considerable volatility quarter to quarter. This should see PCE inflation slow back towards its 2.0%yr
medium-term target as the FOMC desires, from 3.6%yr and 3.1%yr for the headline and core
measures respectively as of September.
Across the Atlantic, the ECB also left its key policy rates unchanged in October. Of note from the
meeting communications, energy prices continue to pose upside risks for inflation, but also downside

risks for growth. "While the full effects of the energy shock are still unfolding, underlying inflation has
remained broadly stable so far and wages do not show a material response". "Financing conditions
have [also] tightened", "which is likely to weigh on inflation and growth". In the press conference,
President Lagarde noted that, between the October and December meetings, a significant amount of
new economic information will be released, allowing forecasts and risks assessments to be tested.

Based on the October meeting communications, we continue to expect a rate hike at the December
meeting but believe this will be prove to be the last hike of the cycle. Note though, the spectrum of risks
Europe faces is broad and skewed towards inflation in the near term, hence the Council’s guidance will
remain hawkish. President Lagarde also announced today that she will step down as ECB President in
February ahead of the scheduled conclusion of her term next October.

## Document 368: IT技术太烂，日本正在被黑客们当怪刷

### Page 1

⽇本企业近期遭遇⿊客攻击潮，短短数周数千万条会员信息⾯临泄露⻛险。 AI Agent 降低
⽹络攻击成本与技术⻔槛，叠加⽇本企业 IT 基础设施⽼旧、内部技术能⼒薄弱及外包依赖
严重，令漏洞利⽤与供应链攻击加速蔓延。
摘要：
“⿊⽇本公司，已经成⼀项运动了 ”
前⼏天， Reddit 上有个⽼哥发了这么⼀句感慨。起因是他刷到⼀⻚⽇本新闻，全都是 “XX 公司
被⿊客攻击，数据可能泄露 ” 。
⽐如  10 ⽉  5 号，连锁烤⾁店 “ 烧⾁  King” 道歉，说⾃家  App 有  1078 万多条会员信息可能泄
漏；

### Page 2

短短  4 天，就有三家公司  3000 万条会员信息存在泄露⻛险。
⽽这，还只是冰⼭⼀⻆。
根据时事通信社和安全⼚商  CS2 汇总，从  9 ⽉  25 ⽇到  10 ⽉  9 ⽇，⽇本已有⼗⼏家知名企业
和机构宣布被⿊客⼊侵、数据疑似泄露。
好好好，现在鞠躬道歉都得组团了。。。
⾯对这场声势浩⼤的 “ ⿊客运动 ” ，⽇本官房⻓官只能表⽰还不清楚事件之间有⽆关联；⽽警察
厅的攻击⼿法调查，据说要到  2027 年  3 ⽉才出结果。
不是哥们⼉，真到那会纪录⽚都出第⼆季了吧。
看到这你可能好奇，怎么突然这么⼀⼤波⿊客⼊侵企业爆发呢？
要世超暴论，背后肯定是  AI。
虽然官⽅还没调查出来⿊客有没有⽤  AI ，但趋势已经很明显了：
⽇本警⽅之前就抓过好⼏个拿  ChatGPT ⼊侵⽇企的⻘少年； 10 ⽉  14 ⽇⽇本安全公司  Scutum
也发⽂实锤，⾄少有  3 个  IP 正在⽤  AI Agent 攻击⽇本企业⽹站，其中⼀个  IP 在  8 ⼩时内丝滑
切换了⼗⼏种⿊客⼯具。

### Page 3

可问题⼜来了， AI ⿊客满世界都是，怎么偏偏逮着⽇本企业往死⾥薅呢？
不好意思，这锅⼀半都得⽇本⼈⾃⼰背。
⾸先，很多⽇本企业的  IT 基础设施⽼旧，安全管理也⻓期落后。
9 ⽉  28 ⽇，漫画界扛把⼦集英社宣布被⿊，原因是旗下博主管理系统的  CMS 配置错误。 CMS
就是⽹站的内容后台，它留了个专⻔⼝⼦给程序对接⽤，本来要先核对⾝份再放⾏，结果设置
出错了，⿊客绕过检查给⾃⼰开了个管理员级别的账号。
就好⽐写字楼的⻔禁办卡机设置错了，本来只有物业能办卡，结果路⼈站过去刷⼀下，就给⾃
⼰办了张全楼通⾏卡。
⽇本警察厅统计，今年上半年就有  123 起勒索软件案，创下有统计以来的最⾼纪录，⽽⼀半受
害者的突破⼝都是  VPN 设备。
说实话，如果你⽤过⽇本⽹站，对这个结果不会意外。
那股扑⾯⽽来的千禧年⽓息，总能让⼈恍惚间以为穿越了回⼆⼗年前。
说直⽩点就是：企业的  IT 技术和意识，⾮常落后。。。
⽇本⽓象厅官⽹

### Page 4

有⽹友吐槽  2026 年了，⽇本很多⽹站的密码还不准⽤  * 、 ! 、 : 等特殊字符，⻓度限制在  8 到  12
位，⽣怕⿊客暴⼒破解时算⼒不够⽤；
还有⼈说有些⼤公司的密码和⽤⼾名⼀模⼀样，⽽且⾄今迷恋⼀种 “PPAP” 古⽼仪式，就是先给
你发⼀封加密  ZIP 压缩包邮件，紧接着再发第⼆封邮件，把解压密码明⽂发给你。
这  TM 和把钥匙压在⻔⼝脚垫下，再贴张纸条 “ 钥匙在脚垫下 ” 有什么区别啊啊？

### Page 5

也有⼈说，某家制造业公司死活不肯升级  ERP 系统（企业管理软件），让员⼯在虚拟机⾥接着
⽤  Windows 7 ，但  Win7 早在  2020 年就停⽌安全更新了啊。。。
看到这你可能会觉得困惑：⽇本不是科技强国吗？
⽇本强的是制造业，像汽⻋、相机、游戏机都能卖到全球；但软件系统，尤其企业⾃⼰内部⽤
的  IT 系统，⼏乎都交给了外包公司。
之前⽇本信息处理推进机构发过⼀组⽩⽪书。
在美国， 65.4% 的  IT ⼈才受雇于各⼤⽤⼾企业（⽐如银⾏、零售商、制造⼚⾃⼰养的技术团
队），只有  34.6% 在外包  IT 公司。
但⽇本完全是反过来的。
2015 年，⽇本有  72% 的  IT ⼈员在外包  IT 公司；到了  2020 年，这个⽐例是  73.6% ，还能
涨？！

### Page 6

这意味着：绝⼤多数⽇本传统⼤企业，内部⼏乎没有研发和运维能⼒。
这种信息不对称和繁琐的对接流程，造成企业在  IT 维护⽅⾯极其落后，⼏乎就是 “ 能⽤就别动
啦 ” 。
那这种⼤环境下，奇葩的密码规则和配置错误就不奇怪了。
更关键的是，这种全员外包的模式，在供应链攻击⾯前就是⼀个超级连环雷：只要⼀家底层外
包商被⿊客攻破，那它⾝上的⼤企业客⼾就会像多⽶诺⻣牌⼀样，⼀个接⼀个倒。
⽐如  10 ⽉  5 ⽇到  6 ⽇，⼤和证券、西铁城前后脚宣布数据可能泄露，就是因为给他们提供客服
系统的同⼀家外包商  被端了。
卡拉  OK 巨头  Big Echo 872 万条信息可能泄露，也是由于外包商⼀名员⼯的电脑中了病毒。
按理说⽇本企业  IT 这么⽼旧、安全管理这么落后，早该出事了，为啥偏偏是现在才集中爆雷
呢？

### Page 7

世超觉得是  AI 模型的能⼒⼜上升了⼀个台阶。
以前，⿊客们压根没那么多精⼒去⼀家家企业破解，哥们费⽼劲肯定是想吃点好的吧？很多企
业就这么靠 “ 性价⽐低 ” 撑到了今天。
但  AI ⼀来，谁跟你俩性价⽐？⿊客多打⼀家的边际成本⼏乎为零。
⽽且，过去那些扫描出漏洞之后的难题，什么利⽤漏洞，⿊⼊后找哪些数据呀、⼯具不管⽤该
换哪个，都得⿊客坐在电脑前⼀步步⼿动操作，⼀个⼈⼀天打不了⼏家。
但现在这些步骤也能交给  AI Agent 了， AI 可以  24 ⼩时⼀家接⼀家试下去。
针对⽇本电商  SQL 注⼊攻击，攻击源  IP 数量两⽉暴涨到  16 倍
其次，AI 也把当⿊客的⻔槛砍没了。
以前当⿊客要懂底层架构、懂汇编、懂漏洞挖掘；现在你只需要懂怎么跟  AI 聊天，就能拥有⼀
个⿊客的⾝份。
前⾯说过，⽇本警⽅已经抓了不少拿  AI 作案的赛博未成年。
2025 年  2 ⽉，东京警视厅逮捕了  3 名只有  14 到  16 岁的初⾼中⽣。他们⽤  AI 写了个⾮法登录
程序，登进别⼈的乐天移动账号，疯狂开通⼿机卡再转⼿倒卖。

12 ⽉，⽇本连锁⽹咖 “ 快活 CLUB” 宣布  729 万名会员信息泄露，后来才发现始作俑者是个  17 岁
的⼤阪男⾼中⽣，⽤  ChatGPT 改进了作案程序，作案群⾥甚⾄还有个⼩学六年级的⼩孩哥。
嗯， AI 实现了科技平权也实现了犯罪平权。
以前你是菜鸡，你只能在键盘上扣  666 ；现在你是菜鸡， AI ⼿把⼿教你写脚本进局⼦。
⼀边是  AI 技术的迅速发展，⼀边是稀烂的  IT 基础设施。
⽇本，就这么成了⿊客的新⼿村。

PROPRIETARY RESEARCH 
EARNINGS AGGREGATES 
STOXX 600 Earnings Outlook provides analysis and commentary on aggregate earnings estimate revisions, growth rates and valuations.

Analyst: Tajinder Dhillon, CFA                                         November 5, 2026 
tajinder.dhillon@lseg.com

STOXX 600 EARNINGS OUTLOOK  
EUROPEAN AGGREGATE ESTIMATES AND REVISIONS

 Third quarter earnings are expected to increase 25.0% from Q3 2026. Excluding the Energy sector, earnings 
are expected to increase 9.5%.

 Third quarter revenue is expected to increase 11.9% from Q3 2026. Excluding the Energy sector, revenues are 
expected to increase 6.9%.

 212 companies in the STOXX 600 have reported earnings to date for Q3 2026. Of these, 58.0% reported results 
exceeding analyst estimates.

 257 companies in the STOXX 600 have reported revenue to date for Q3 2026. Of these, 64.2% reported revenue 
exceeding analyst estimates.

 During the week of November 9, 40 companies are expected to report quarterly earnings.

Through November 5, 212 companies in the STOXX 600 Index reported earnings for Q3 2026. Of these, 58% reported 
earnings above analyst estimates. In a typical quarter (sin ce 2012), 54% of companies beat estimates and 39% miss 
estimates.

In aggregate, companies are reporting earnings that are 4.2% above estimates, which is below the 5.9% long-term 
(since 2012) average surprise factor.

The estimated earnings growth rate for the STOXX 600 fo r Q3 2026 is 25%. The STOXX 600 expects to see share-
weighted earnings of €164.4B in Q3 2026, compared to shar e-weighted earnings of €131.6B (based on the year-ago 
earnings of the current constituents) in Q3 2025.

Eight of the ten sectors in the index expect to see an improvement in earnings relative to Q3 2025. The Energy sector 
has the highest earnings growth rate for the quarter, wh ile the Real Estate has the weakest anticipated growth 
compared to Q3 2025.

The Energy sector has the highest earnings growth rate (153 .4%) of any sector. It is expected to earn €35.7B in Q3 
2026, compared to earnings of €14.1B in Q3 2025.

The Real Estate sector has the lowest earnings growth rate (-69.6%) of any sector. It is expected to earn €0.9B in Q3 
2026, compared to earnings of €3.1B in Q3 2025.

The estimated revenue growth rate for the STOXX 600 for Q3 2026 is 11.9%. The STOXX 600 is expected to earn 
revenues of €1581.6B in Q3 2026, compared to €1413.6B in Q3 2025.

Eight of the ten sectors in the index expect to see an improvement in revenues relative to Q3 2025. The Energy 
sector has the highest revenue growth rate for the quarter, while the Consumer Cyclicals has the weakest anticipated 
growth compared to Q3 2025.

The Energy sector has the highest revenue growth rate (39.4%) of any sector. It is expected to generate sales of 
€302.7B in Q3 2026, compared to sales of €217.2B in Q3 2025.

The Consumer Cyclicals sector has the lowest revenue growth rate (-1.2%) of any sector. It is expected to generate 
sales of €196.8B in Q3 2026, compared to sales of €199.1B in Q3 2025.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS  
SEPTEMBER CPI PREVIEW
Setting the pace for the run into year end
19 October 2026
Key points
• September CPI to rise 0.62%mth 
with the annual pace lifting to 4.1%yr 
from 4.0%yr. The seasonally adjusted 
CPI lifts 0.5%mth, a step down from 
the 0.7% increase in August.  
• September Trimmed Mean (TM) is 
set to lift 0.26%mth holding the 
annual pace steady at 3.6%yr. 
• As we noted in our review of the 
August CPI, our September quarter 
CPI estimate is unchanged at 1.3%qtr 
while the TM remained 0.9%qtr.  
• Auto fuel surged ahead of our 
expectations so along with signs of 
increase pass-through we lifted our 
December quarter CPI to 1.3%qtr/3.9%yr 
and the TM estimate to 0.9%qtr/3.5%yr. 
• This is a 0.8ppt increase to the CPI 
estimate, and a 0.2ppt increase to the 
TM estimate, highlighting inflation 
momentum meaningfully above 
the RBA’s current expectations.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
August inflation came in soft...
Inflation moderated in August following July’s strong 
outcome. The CPI rose 0.4% in the month, in line with our 
expectation but slightly below market consensus of 0.5%, 
taking the annual rate to 4.0%. On a seasonally adjusted 
basis, the CPI increased 0.7%mth, underscoring the impact of 
seasonal discounting in August and the start of the financial 
year repricing in July.
More closely watched are the measures of underlying inflation 
including the Trimmed Mean (TM) and market services. 
The TM rose 0.2% in August, in line with our expectations 
but again softer than the market, leaving the annual pace 
unchanged at 3.6% for a third consecutive month. Meanwhile, 
market services excluding volatiles & holiday travel increased 
0.2%mth/4.1%yr. Notable strength (three-month annualised, 
seasonally adjusted) was seen in sports participation (10.9%), 
clothing repair (7.0%), restaurant meals (6.8%) and other 
household services (5.2%). For full analysis of the August CPI, 
see “August CPI – Came in on our expectations ”.
The expenditure-weighted share of the CPI basket 
experiencing positive price shocks eased to 11% in August 
from 15% in July, while the share experiencing negative price 
shocks declined from 17% to just below 13%. The majority of 
the positive shocks were concentrated in consumer durables 
On a three-month moving average basis, the net momentum 
share (positive minus negative) rose to –4.5% from –7.7% 
in July and a trough of –15.8% in May, suggesting the 
disinflationary impulse may be nearing exhaustion. 
... with September due for a moderate pick-up
Looking ahead to September, early indicators painted a 
mixed picture. The NAB business survey showed retail price 
growth slowing to 0.9%qtr from 1.1%qtr in August, with 
labour cost growth easing to 1.7%qtr from 1.8%qtr, while 
purchase cost growth edged up to 2.4%qtr from 2.3%qtr. 
Nevertheless, all measures remain well above their long-run 
averages while the gap between purchase costs and final 
prices continues to widen, indicating businesses are facing 
margin compression (see here for further detail). Meanwhile, 
consumers’ year-ahead inflation expectations held steady 
at 4.9% in September (easing slightly at 2dp) but surged to 
5.3% in October likely reflecting higher fuel prices, with retail 
petrol and diesel averaging $2.20/l (+8.2%mth) and $2.68/l 
(+7.7%mth). We do believe the auto fuel prices are likely to see 
a near term peak in October.  Meanwhile, global shipping costs 
have also surged recently amid renewed Middle East tensions.
Sep quarter unrevised but Dec upgraded significantly
Justin Smirk, Senior Economist
Neha Sharma, Economist

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
The September CPI is expected to rise 0.6% (0.62% to 2dp)
with the annual pace lifting to 4.1%yr. Our seasonally adjusted
estimate increases 0.5% (0.48% to 2dp). For the monthly
TM, we expect a 0.3% (0.26% to 2dp) lift, holding the annual

pace steady at 3.6%. On a 3m/3m basis, the TM is tracking
a 1.0% gain in September (noting that while this measure is
conceptually different to the quarterly TM it still serves as a
useful guide). For market services excluding volatiles & holiday
travel we have pencilled in another 0.2% rise, slowing the
annual pace slightly to 4.0% (a nine-month low).
The largest contributors are recreation & culture (+0.27ppt),
driven wholly by holiday travel & accommodation. Transport

(+0.23ppt) makes a solid contribution, with gains in fuel
(+0.26ppt) partly offset by motor vehicles (–0.02ppt). Housing
also contributes (+0.13ppt), with rents and new dwellings
each adding 0.02ppt while property rates add 0.08ppt.
The key detractors in the month are food & non-alcoholic
beverages (–0.08ppt) and garments (–0.04ppt).
Key details for the month of September
Food & non-alcoholic beverages are expected to fall
0.5%mth, easing the annual pace to 2.8%yr. The weakness is

concentrated in a 3.7%mth decline in fruit & vegetables, while
non-alcoholic beverages are also expected to fall 1.5%mth.
Meals out & takeaway is forecast to rise 0.1%mth, easing the
annual rate to 3.9%.
Housing is expected to rise 0.6% in September, slowing the
annual pace to 5.5%yr. Component-wise:
• Rents are expected to rise 0.3%mth and 3.7%yr. CPI rent
inflation is nearing its peak, consistent with the moderation
in asking rent growth. We expect rent inflation to ease to

3.6%yr by end-2026 and 2.5%yr by end-2027.
• New dwelling prices, which capture only the building
component of construction costs, are expected to rise
0.2%mth and 4.9%yr. Ongoing weakness in established
housing markets would be increasing builders’ difficulty in
passing through higher costs to new builds. As a result, we
expect new dwelling inflation to moderate to 4.5%yr by
end-2026 and 2.1%yr by end-2027.
Transport inflation is expected to rise 2.0%mth in September,

lifting the annual pace to 7.7%yr reflecting an 8.3%mth
increase in fuel prices following higher oil prices through the
month. Motor vehicle prices and spare parts are expected to
fall 0.7%mth and 1.3%mth, respectively. In NSW, a one-year
vehicle registration discount between $80-100 came into
effect from 1 September, which is likely to weigh on the other
services in respect of motor vehicles component.
Recreation & culture is expected to rise 2.1%mth driven by a

4.4%mth increase in holiday travel, partly offset by a 0.4%
decline in audio, visual & computing equipment & services and
a 1.3% fall in sporting services.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
Risks to September baseline
We view the risks around the September CPI outcome as broadly 
balanced. Waning spending momentum outside of fuel may make 
it difficult for businesses to pass through higher costs in the near 
term. However, some of these pricing pressures may be deferred 
rather than forgone. The October ban on card surcharging may 
prompt a greater-than-usual degree of price resetting, allowing 
businesses to recover some of the margin pressures that have built 
up in recent months.
Risks to housing are mixed. Trade materials suppliers continued 
to announce above-average price increases through September 
(around 8%). While soft conditions in the established housing 
market may limit the extent to which these costs are passed 
through to new dwelling prices, they nevertheless present an 
upside risk to housing-related inflation, particularly maintenance 
& repair and insurance costs. Growth in advertised rents also 
continues to ease but these new leases take some time to flow 
through to average rents paid measured in the CPI. 
A kick up in October ...
We have a solid preliminary estimate for the October CPI of 
0.6%mth lifting the annual pace to 4.8%yr. It is true that the key 
driver is an 8.9% increase in auto fuel but we estimate the TM to 
lift 0.4%mth/3.7%yr highlighting broader inflationary pressures. 
We expect that the removal of credit charge surcharges and 
resulting increase in retail prices, at most, will contribute 0.1ppt 
but only just so see it as more of a rounding error. Nevertheless, 
these changes come at a time of a significant upstream price 
pressure, particularly for SMEs, so there is some risk of broader 
price adjustments. As such, we expect marginally larger than 
usual price adjustments in October particularly in hospitality 
and household services. Being the first month of the quarter the 
October lift sets a higher base for the quarterly average estimates 
resulting in an upgrade to our December quarter estimate.
We have included a table with a monthly profile for the CPI, 
seasonally adjusted CPI and the TM out to June 2027. At 
June 2027 we have an annual pace of 3.7%yr for the CPI and 
seasonally adjusted CPI and 3.3%yr for the TM. There is also a 
table breaking down the monthly components of the CPI through 
the December quarter. 
... highlights stronger inflationary pressure ...
Our September quarter CPI estimate is unrevised at 1.3%qtr 
with the annual pace steady at 3.9%yr. At the margin there 
was a 0.04ppt increase due to a lift in auto fuel but this is 
1.29%qtr at two decimal places so still rounds to 1.3%. 
The seasonally adjusted CPI gains 1.1% in the September 
quarter highlighting that part of the increase is due to usual 
seasonality. Our TM estimate for Q3 has a small downward 
revision of 0.03ppt to 0.88% at two decimal place so our 
published estimate is also unrevised at 0.9%qtr. 
... resulting in an upward revision to Q4 2026
Westpac’s December quarter CPI estimate has been boosted 
from a 0.5%qtr to 1.3%qtr. Driving most of this change from 
our previous estimate is the 15.3% increase in autofuel, it

was 1.9%, contributing 0.55ppt to the 0.8ppt increase in the

A disinflationary impulse kicks in for 2027
We now expect auto fuel prices to peak in October (240 cpl 
for petrol, 284 cpl for diesel) and then to trend lower through 
2027 (184 cpl for petrol and 194 cpl for diesel by December 
2027) leading to a 13.6% falling in auto fuel in the first half 
of 2027 subtracting 0.46ppt off the CPI over the same period 
of time. For a detailed description on how we constructed the 
auto fuels profile please see: “ Persistent energy pressures 
have further to run ” 
The first half of 2027 will also see an almost 5% decline in 
holiday travel & accommodation which will subtract a further 
0.29ppt off the CPI. However, outside of that any broader 
moderation in inflation is expected to be more muted hence 
we have the TM printing 0.7%qtr for each quarter in 2027.  
Critical to this moderation in core inflation is our expect step 
down in dwelling price inflation on the back of falling estab -
lished home prices. From a 1.1%qtr increase in Q3 2026 we 
see dwelling price increasing by 0.7% in the Q4 2026, and 
then 0.6% in both Q1 and Q2 2027 before dropping to 0.5%qtr 
in Q3 and Q4 2027. This will see dwelling price inflation in the 
CPI drop from 5.4%yr at June 2026 to 3.0%yr by June 2027 
then down to 2.2%yr by December 2027. 
At least for the near term a key uncertainty remains the extent 
to which businesses are able to pass through ongoing labour 
and non-labour cost pressures and how this unwinds through 
2027 as energy prices ease and the domestic economy slows 
under the weight of higher interest rates and falling estab -
lished home prices. There is a risk that firms face greater 
resistance to price increases if discretionary household spend -
ing softens by more than expected. That said, these headwinds 
may be at least partially offset by the stronger starting point 
for household incomes, which has proven more resilient than 
previously anticipated, and by higher energy prices due to the 
ongoing conflict in the Middle East.
That said, there is also the risk that the disinflationary pulse by 
the second half of 2027 could be greater than what we antici-

level if not lower, then headline inflation could dip below 2%yr 
and the TM would be closer to 2.5%yr (the mid point if the 
RBA’s inflation target), by end 2027.

## Document 466: Westpac Wave - Fiji Quarterly Economic Update (2026-11-07)

2
WESTPAC PACIFIC ECONOMICS
FIJI ECONOMIC UPDATE
Fiji’s resilience is being tested...
Fiji’s economy entered 2026 with growth already normalising
from the post-pandemic recovery. Real GDP growth slowed from
3.6% in 2024 to 2.5% in 2025. Foreign reserves started the year

around $3.7bn, banking-system liquidity remained high and the
RBF maintained its Overnight Policy Rate at 0.25%.
Activity so far into 2026 has been somewhat stronger than what
we feared. Visitor arrivals reached 762,352 during January–
September, up 3.7%yr, while construction, investment lending,
cement sales, remittances and private-sector credit remained
firm. Nevertheless, Westpac Fiji has kept its 2026 growth
forecast unchanged at 2.0%. Stronger headline indicators are

not translating one-for-one into real economic growth because
higher prices and intermediate costs are absorbing a growing
share of nominal activity.
The outlook changed materially in March as the Middle East
conflict disrupted global energy markets. Fiji remains heavily
dependent on imported petroleum products, so higher refined-
fuel, freight and insurance costs flowed through transport,
electricity, food distribution and wider business expenses. By

June, motor spirit price reached $3.93/litre and diesel at a record
$4.58/litre, while automotive-fuel sales value rose 24.4%yr in the
June quarter.
As this report goes out, the Middle East conflict remains a
key external downside risk. At least 12 vessels were attacked
in the week ending 16 October and yet it is reported that oil
flows through Hormuz averaged 11.6mn barrels per day to 13
October, about 68% of pre-war levels through alternative and

costly routes. Iran says a seven-day reopening proposal remains
available, but US-Iran negotiations remain unresolved.
Westpac Economics’ most recent scenario analysis “ Persistent
energy pressures have further to run ” indicates that relief will
be gradual. Brent crude is forecast to average US$101/bbl in Q4
2026 before easing through 2027. Refined products are expected
to normalise more slowly because refinery capacity, inventories,
freight and insurance remain constrained. Under Westpac’s

escalation scenario, in a return to open conflict Brent could reach
around US$150/bbl in H1 2027.
Domestic tourism sector demonstrates the nominal-versus-
real growth tension. June-quarter visitor arrivals increased to
287,588, yet earnings fell 4.9%yr to $708.1mn. First-half tourism
earnings declined 1.7%yr despite higher arrivals, even though
hotel takings increased 9.5%yr.
Australia and New Zealand remain critical, together contributing
around 71% of September arrivals. Westpac expects Australian

growth to slow from 2.1% in 2026 to 1.5% in 2027, while New
Zealand strengthens from 1.9% to 2.6%. Tourism therefore
increasingly needs to focus on yield rather than visitor numbers
alone.
Shamal Chand
Senior Economist, Westpac Pacific Economics

### Page 3

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
3
WESTPAC PACIFIC ECONOMICS
FIJI ECONOMIC UPDATE
...but the buffers are still doing the heavy lifting...
There are good reasons why growth held up despite these shocks.
Construction work put in place reached $155.9mn in the June
quarter, up 8.6%yr, while investment lending, cement sales and

construction-related imports subsequently remained strong.
Industrial production increased 1.5%yr, although performance
remained uneven across industries.
Inward personal remittances have been particularly impressive,
reaching $1.2bn during January–August, up 32.7%yr. We expect
remittances to reach $1.55bn this year. These inflows, with strong
diaspora support, are helping households absorb higher living
costs while simultaneously supporting Fiji’s external position.

However, the external accounts show the cost of this resilience.
Fiji’s merchandise trade deficit widened to $2.9bn in the first half,
with imports reaching $4.3bn against exports of $1.4bn. Mineral-
fuel imports alone reached around $1.2bn, up 57%yr. Foreign
reserves nevertheless recovered to approximately $4.09bn by
end-September, equivalent to 5.7 months of retained imports , also
supported by Government’s external loan draw-down.
Our in-house import-demand indicator is signalling softer

domestic momentum. Import activity over the three months
to September was down 21.3%yr and fell 39.8% compared
with the preceding three months. The broader six-month
measure declined at a more moderate 16.1% annualised pace.
While volatile, the trend adds to our view that higher prices are
weighing on real activity, despite continued strength across
tourism, remittances, credit and construction.
Headline inflation reached 7.6%yr in August before easing to

6.8% in September, with transport prices up 12.2%yr and food
prices up 7.6%. Our latest forecast has November inflation
around 8.0%yr, average inflation around 4.3% for 2026 and
December (year-end) inflation at 7.4%.
Higher fuel prices are no longer affecting only households.
The shock is moving through freight, electricity, agriculture,
construction materials and wider supply chains. For instance,
we note recent increases in taxi and bus fares. These higher

intermediate costs compress business margins and reduce
household purchasing power.
This also informs our 2027 view. Westpac Fiji has downgraded
its 2027 growth forecast to 2.5%, reflecting the prevailing
effects of the energy crisis, elevated refined-fuel prices and the
broader transmission of higher intermediate costs into next year.
Westpac’s global outlook supports this caution: crude prices are
expected to ease gradually, but refined products could remain

comparatively expensive for longer.
Renewable energy is increasingly important because the fuel
crisis has put a clear economic price on Fiji’s imported-energy
dependence. The faster Fiji prioritises a shift to renewable energy,
the sooner it can establish a more stable energy foundation.
El Niño creates an additional risk through agriculture, hydro
generation and food prices. The buffers remain meaningful, but
they are being asked to work significantly harder.

### Page 4

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
4
WESTPAC PACIFIC ECONOMICS
FIJI ECONOMIC UPDATE
...as risks converge
Government finances add another dimension to the outlook.
The FY2025/26 deficit came in at $597.9mn, or 4.3% of GDP,
substantially better than originally budgeted.
Nevertheless, financing requirements are substantial.

Government plans to raise $1.48bn in FY2026/27, comprising
$1.046bn in deficit financing and $436.3mn refinancing. Around
$853.9mn to be domestically sourced and $627.95mn externally,
including a $444mn ADB policy-based loan in November.
Domestic yields therefore deserve closer attention. Fiji’s
government yield curve has steepened considerably, with the
adjustment concentrated beyond 10 years. While the 10-year
yield remained at 3.90%, the 20-year yield rose by 80 basis points

between July 2025 and September 2026 to 5.80%. The 25-year
yield increased even more sharply, climbing from 5.94% to 7.19%
after peaking at 7.25% in mid-2026.
Pressure is also emerging at the short end. The Government’s
20 October Treasury Bill tender offered $50mn across three-,
six- and 12-month maturities. Investors bid for the full amount,
but sought yields of 0.35%, 0.60% and 1.30%, respectively,
above the listed yields of 0.30%, 0.55% and 1.25%. Government

did not accept the higher yields, leaving the entire $50mn offer
unallocated. This suggests investors are increasingly seeking
better returns even on shorter-dated Government securities.
The financial system still provides considerable protection.
Banking-system liquidity stood around $2.28bn at end-
September and the RBF maintained its OPR at 0.25%. In August,
the weighted-average lending rate was 4.51%, the savings-
deposit rate 0.31% and the time-deposit rate 2.01%.

For now, high liquidity limits immediate crowding-out risks.
Higher government yields may encourage banks and institutional
investors to allocate more funds towards government securities.
If liquidity subsequently tightens, deposit rates may need to rise
to retain savings, adding pressure to bank funding costs and
eventually lending rates.
S&P’s decision to maintain Fiji’s B+/B ratings but revise the
outlook from stable to negative reinforces the importance of

debt sustainability, investor confidence and credible medium-
term fiscal consolidation.
Political uncertainty adds another layer, with ongoing public
debate on constitutional amendments and the general election
due by 6 February 2027.
This brings us back to the growth story. We have maintained
2026 growth at 2.0% because resilient tourism, remittances,
construction and investment are being offset by unexpectedly
high inflation and intermediate costs. Our downgrade of 2027

growth to 2.5% recognises that this adjustment will take longer
than previously expected, while fiscal, financial, energy, climate
and political risks increasingly overlap.

WESTPAC PACIFIC ECONOMICS
INFLATION OUTLOOK
Cost pressures move beyond the pump
Inflation has emerged as a key pressure point for Fiji, threatening 
to erode gains from otherwise resilient domestic activity. 
Headline inflation accelerated to 6.8%yr in September, driven by 
essentials: food prices rose 7.6%yr, transport increased 12.2%yr 
and housing and utilities were 4.9% higher. The underlying 
picture remains less severe, with prices declining 0.4%mth in 
September and annual-average inflation at 1.9%. Nevertheless, 
the acceleration suggests the global fuel shock is increasingly 
working its way through the domestic economy.

The import-price pipeline reinforces this view. Import prices 
increased 4.9%qtr and 4.4%yr in the June quarter, including 
a 15.4%yr rise in mineral-product prices as petrol, diesel and 
aviation-fuel costs increased. Export prices rose 6.2%yr, although 
the terms of trade eased 0.6%qtr. Elevated energy costs are 
also spreading beyond the pump. Transport-services producer 
prices increased 2.7%qtr in June, while building-material prices 
rose 1.5%qtr in September, signalling broader pressure across 
distribution, construction and services.

Fuel-price pressures intensified again in October, when regulated 
unleaded prices in Viti Levu increased to $3.15/litre and diesel 
rose by 21 cents to $3.41/litre. Prices nevertheless remain below 
their June peaks. Our indicative estimates point to further 
increases in fuel prices over the coming months.

The global outlook offers little immediate relief. Westpac 
Economics expects Brent to average US$101/bbl in Q4 2026 
before easing to US$77/bbl by Q4 2027, but refined fuels are 
expected to ease more slowly as refinery supply remains more 
constrained, while shipping, insurance and inventory pressures 
gradually normalise. Westpac’s Australian forecasts provide 
some regional context, with diesel and petrol expected to 
average 274c/l and 230c/l, respectively, in Q4 before declining 
through 2027.

For Fiji, the distinction between crude and refined-product prices 
is important. Singapore gasoil prices, freight and insurance costs 
are expected to remain elevated and ease only gradually through 
2027, meaning lower Brent prices may not translate immediately 
into cheaper imported fuel. Higher diesel costs feed into freight, 
construction, agriculture and distribution, while aviation-
fuel pressures add to airline operating costs and tourism-
sector margins. Repeated shocks increase the likelihood that 
businesses eventually pass more of these costs on to consumers, 
particularly through food and other essentials.

We consequently expect inflation to remain elevated through 
year-end, rising towards 8.0% in November before easing to 7.4% 
in December, taking annual-average inflation to around 4.3%. 
The concern is increasingly that an imported fuel shock develops 
into broader cost-push inflation into next year. The squeeze on 
real purchasing power makes inflation an increasingly important 
downside risk to Fiji’s growth outlook heading into 2027.
Source: FBoS, Macrobond, Westpac Economics

### Page 7

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
7
WESTPAC PACIFIC ECONOMICS
CREDIT RATING OUTLOOK
S&P negative outlook is concerning
Fiji’s sovereign credit profile has come under renewed pressure
recently, adding another layer of caution to an economy already
navigating softer real growth and elevated external risks.

S&P maintained Fiji’s long-term rating at B+ and short-term
rating at B but revised the outlook from Stable to Negative.
While not a downgrade, it signals increased risk if fiscal
and debt metrics deteriorate. Historically, four of Fiji’s six
negative outlooks since 2006 have resulted in downgrades,
typically during periods of major economic or fiscal stress.
Similar vulnerabilities are resurfacing, with rising government
expenditure and guarantees, alongside Middle East tensions and

inflationary pressures.
The key concern is fiscal. S&P viewed the 2026/27 Budget as
more expansionary than previously expected, effectively slowing
the fiscal consolidation achieved in previous years. It projects
the fiscal deficit to average 4.6% of GDP over 2027–29, including
4.9% in FY2027, with net government debt moving above 70% of
GDP. Once contingent liabilities are incorporated, the broader
public-sector exposure approaches 80% of GDP. This leaves

considerably less fiscal space to absorb another major domestic
or external shock.
In S&P’s latest assessment, contingent liabilities are becoming
increasingly important to the sovereign risk story. Government
exposure through guarantees and other contingent liabilities
adds to the broader fiscal risk, while a widening current account
deficit, elevated imports and rising external debt reinforce the
risks surrounding the outlook. For Fiji, this matters because

the next phase of growth will require substantial investment in
infrastructure, climate resilience and productive capacity.
The negative outlook therefore strengthens the case for a more
credible medium-term fiscal consolidation path by the Fiji
Government. S&P indicated that the outlook could return to
Stable if economic growth and tourism outperform its forecasts,
resulting in narrower fiscal deficits and lower debt ratios, or if
improvements in institutional and policy settings sustainably

strengthen fiscal outcomes and debt levels.
Importantly, the assessment across rating agencies is not
uniformly negative. Moody’s maintained Fiji’s B1 long-term issuer
rating with a Stable outlook, suggesting that despite significant
structural and fiscal challenges, the overall credit profile remains
broadly unchanged and risks remain balanced.
A future downgrade would likely reinforce perceptions of
higher sovereign risk just as Fiji requires greater private and

foreign investment. Maintaining fiscal credibility, containing
contingent liabilities and ensuring that new borrowing supports
productivity-enhancing investment will be increasingly
important. With elevated uncertainty, climate vulnerability and
external shocks remaining additional downside risks, the room
for further fiscal slippage is becoming increasingly limited for the
Fijian Government.

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
8
WESTPAC PACIFIC ECONOMICS
SUPER EL NIÑO
The threat of a “very strong” El Niño
It is the year of the ‘very strong’ El Niño, and for Fiji the risk is no
longer just on the horizon. Dry conditions are already affecting
parts of the country, while the Government has stepped up

preparations for a potentially more difficult period ahead.
History suggests this matters for the broader economy because
prolonged drought can simultaneously affect agriculture, water
supply and electricity generation before flowing through food
prices, imports, rural incomes and ultimately growth.
Our rainfall analysis covering 1981–2025 shows that rainfall
during El Niño periods was 24.7% lower than under neutral
conditions nationally, with all four regions recording historical

deficits above 20%.
During the 1982–83 El Niño, severe drought coincided with
Cyclone Oscar and a weak global economy. Historical estimates
highlight that Fiji’s economy contracted by 4.0%, against a
pre-disaster forecast of 2.7% growth during the super El Niño
period then. The 1997–98 episode was more severe. By 1998 the
cane crop was forecast at just 1.88mn tonnes, producing around
150,000 tonnes of sugar. Historical estimates used here indicate

cane production fell around 26%, contributing to an aggregate
GDP contraction of at least 1.3%. Fiji also devalued the dollar by
20% in January 1998 amid the overlapping Asian Financial Crisis.
The social cost was significant. In 1998, around 27,562
households needed food relief and 47,592 were already receiving
emergency water supplies according to some surveys. The 2015–
16 episode again brought crop losses and drying water supplies.
The effects are beginning to show

The current event is already leaving a footprint. September
rainfall was drier than usual across the Western, Central and
Eastern divisions and most of the Northern Division, while several
Western Division stations recorded less than 100mm.
More recent monitoring points to serious or very dry conditions
across parts of Vanua Levu, Taveuni, Lomaiviti, Yasawa, Lau and
other areas. Local effects are becoming tangible. Taveuni had
already required assistance with water carting in September,

while NDRMO reported that Fiji was experiencing meteorological
drought and warned it could progress towards hydrological and
agricultural drought if conditions continued.
The Government’s response has consequently shifted towards
preparedness. The National Disaster Risk Management Council
is reviewing the National El Niño Preparedness and Response
Plan. Humanitarian clusters were also instructed to prepare
contingency plans for consolidation and presentation to Cabinet,

while NDRMO has been working with Energy Fiji Limited and the
Water Authority of Fiji to assess emerging risks.
This marks a progression from earlier in the year. In August,
our assessment was that the FY2026–27 Budget contained
no explicit El Niño preparedness measure, implying much
of the initial response would need to occur through existing
institutional arrangements.
The economic transmission remains important. Agriculture feels
reduced rainfall first through weaker soil moisture and water

availability. Lower domestic food production can then lift prices
and import requirements. Electricity provides another channel
as weaker hydro generation could require increased thermal
generation and imported fuel, adding to higher production
costs and foreign-exchange outflows. And Fiji has less room for
another supply-side shock.
Fiji is more diversified than during the droughts of the 1980s
and 1990s, while its reserve position provides an important

buffer. But financial buffers cannot replace rainfall, agricultural
production or water supplies.

WESTPAC PACIFIC ECONOMICS
RESEARCH INSIGHTS
Why is ‘foreign reserves’ so important for Fiji?
Foreign reserves remain one of the most closely watched indica -
tors of Fiji’s economic health. At the end of September, reserves 
stood at around $4.1 billion, equivalent to 5.7 months of retained 
imports. The figure features prominently in Reserve Bank report -
ing, and for good reason. 
For a small, import-dependent economy operating a managed 
exchange-rate regime, reserves provide an important buffer 
against external shocks.
In simple terms, foreign reserves can be thought of as Fiji’s 
foreign-currency savings account. Fiji earns foreign exchange 
through tourism, remittances, exports, foreign investment and 
external borrowing. At the same time, foreign currency is required 
to pay for imports, service external debt and meet other overseas 
obligations. When foreign-currency inflows exceed outflows, 
reserves generally rise. When the reverse occurs, reserves come 
under pressure.

The analogy is not perfect, but it highlights why reserves matter. 
Unlike some larger economies, Fiji cannot simply rely on its ex -
change rate to absorb external shocks. The Fiji dollar is managed 
against a basket comprising the Australian, US and New Zealand 
dollars, the euro and the Japanese yen. This provides a degree 
of exchange-rate stability and predictability for households and 
businesses, but it also means that foreign reserves are needed to 
effectively provide a shock absorber for the economy.

There is also an important connection with domestic financial 
conditions. Our analysis suggests that before July 2020, every 
$1 million increase in reserves was associated with around $0.31 
million in additional banking-system liquidity. Since then, the 
relationship has strengthened substantially to around $0.83 mil -
lion. Following the pandemic, external borrowing, grants, remit -
tances and the recovery in tourism helped lift foreign-exchange 
inflows and thus banking liquidity, while government expenditure 
and limited sterilisation further contributed to abundant domes -
tic liquidity.

This highlights an important policy trade-off. Economists refer to 
the “impossible trinity”, where an economy cannot simultaneous -
ly maintain a fixed or tightly managed exchange rate, completely 
free capital movement and fully independent monetary policy. 
Fiji’s framework therefore relies on a combination of foreign re -
serves, monetary-policy settings, exchange controls and broader 
market management to maintain stability.

Those controls, however, are not without cost. Restrictions on 
moving capital offshore limit the flexibility available to house -
holds, foreign investors and Fiji-based businesses. 
A deeper question is therefore whether Fiji could eventually move 
towards a more flexible exchange-rate framework.

Probably, but not yet. Greater exchange-rate flexibility could 
theoretically allow the currency itself to absorb more of an 
external shock. But that requires considerably deeper financial 
markets than what Fiji presently has. Foreign-exchange trading 
remains relatively shallow, secondary-market activity in govern -
ment securities is limited, while instruments that allow business -
es to hedge currency risk are significantly underdeveloped.

There is also an important transmission issue. A weaker Fiji dollar 
could theoretically improve export competitiveness, but the 
benefits would take time. Merchandise exports remain con -
strained by domestic production capacity, while tourism is also 
influenced by available capacity and advance bookings. Higher 
import prices, by contrast, would flow through much faster. Given 
Fiji’s heavy reliance on imported fuel, food, machinery and other 
goods, currency depreciation would quickly add to inflation and 
business costs.

The experiences of Australia and Singapore offer two useful per -
spectives.
 Australia floated its dollar in 1983 alongside broader finan -
cial-sector liberalisation and market development. Singapore 
followed a different path. Despite possessing a highly developed 
financial system, it continues to manage its currency against a 
basket, with the exchange rate occupying a central role in mone -
tary policy.

For Fiji, the lesson is not that one exchange-rate regime is nec -
essarily superior to another. Rather, greater flexibility requires 
deeper markets and stronger transmission mechanisms. Devel -
oping the foreign-exchange market, domestic capital markets 
and currency-risk management instruments therefore needs to 
precede any significant shift in the present regime.

Until then, foreign reserves will remain much more than another 
number in the RBF’s monthly statistics. It acts as a buffer against 
external shocks, help underpin confidence in the Fiji dollar and 
provide the economy with room to continue paying for essential 
imports when foreign-currency inflows weaken. At $4.1 billion, 
the reserve position remains comfortable, but maintaining that 
buffer remains fundamental to Fiji’s macroeconomic stability .

西、加拿大等新興市場因 semaglutide專利到期 ，

《醫療器械喜憂參半，但必要性手術需求仍佳》
近月醫療服務設備股為健護中股價表現較差 ，主因：
1)亞培受到產品責任險達成部分和解協議 ，後續還
有其他聯邦 、州判決仍為股價壓力； 2)波士頓科學
受資安事件影響生產宣告無法達成財測 ，HRS的左

心耳封堵術 (LAAC) 指南適用 WATCHMAN、 亞培
Amulet，可同時做消融+LAAC，但LAAC不能完全
替代口服抗凝血藥物 (OAC)對預防中風的效用 ，仍
為二線，等同喜憂參半 。另受自付成本增加 ，預期
擇期、外科手術需求疲軟延伸至年底 ，機器人手術
成長率區間接近 14.5%，下半年低於上半年 ，將以
擴大翻新系統的布署降低門檻及增加門診手術需求 ，
擴大目標市場 ，但必要醫療手術需求健康 ，心血管
類手術下半年仍成長，醫療器材基本面喜憂參半。
《星級獎金與醫療成本控管力使長期獲利分化》
每月聯邦醫療保險優勢計畫投保人數成長趨勢持續 ，
CMS預計於2026年10月中公布 2027年醫療優勢計
畫(MA)星級評等，因評級變嚴將影響醫保公司會員
留存率、競爭力及2028年品質獎金收入不確定性增
加，低於4星級業者面臨品質獎金減少與長期獲利承
壓風險。另無意外法案獨立爭議解決 (IDR)仲裁成本
持續推升商業保險理賠支出 ，增加2027年雇主團體
及ACA市場保費定價壓力 。管理式醫療業者今年展

現利潤優先於市占率、砍不賺錢計畫/人數拉抬利潤
率，但明年若保費調漲幅度不及醫療成本增速 ，恐
推升醫療損失率 、壓縮承保利潤 。後續獲利關鍵在
於星級評等 、保費調整能力及理賠成本控管 ，業者
間的長期獲利表現可能進一步分化。

## Document 513: 【趨勢主題】歐洲市場問答：高利環境下的壓力與機會

Source PDF: all report pdf/【趨勢主題】歐洲市場問答：高利環境下的壓力與機會.pdf
Report source: CTBC
Date: 2026-10-02

歐洲市場問答：高利環境下的壓力與機會
1
Thematic Report
重點提示
中東地緣衝突自2026年2月底爆發以來仍陷僵局，能源價格維
持高檔的時間較預期更長，令歐元區通膨壓力與升息預期升溫。
另一方面，歐洲政府擴大財政支出，公債供給增加，加上歐洲
央行持續縮表，推升期限溢價，令歐洲公債殖利率大幅走升，
德國十年期公債殖利率9/28升破3.6%，創2009年6月以來新高。
本報告透過問答，分析歐洲公債殖利率走高的原因、主權債務
與財政風險、政府因應措施、高利率對企業的影響，以及高利
率環境下的歐洲股債展望。
◼ 本波歐洲公債殖利率走高的驅動因素：能源價格上漲推升通
膨、市場重估歐央貨幣政策路徑，同時，財政支出令歐洲公

債供給增加與歐央持續縮表使私人部門需要吸收更多公債供
給，推升期限溢價，財政疑慮使部分歐洲國家利差承受額外
壓力，加上全球利率上升的外溢影響，共同推升殖利率走高。
◼ 歐洲政府調整發債結構，央行備有穩市工具：歐洲政府縮短
發債期限以控制融資成本，預估此趨勢將延續至2027年；
萬一主權債市場出現無序波動，歐洲央行有OMT與TPI政策
工具，但不會輕易動用。
◼ 整體歐洲債務風險尚可控但中長期壓力持續累積：目前利率
水準尚未對整體債務可持續性構成普遍威脅，但財政脆弱性
較高的國家壓力升高，法國尤其需要關注。財政與政治變數
使法國資產風險溢價維持高位，目前股債定價已反映相當程
度的負面風險。
◼ 德國公債殖利率短期將維持高檔：歐洲央行12月仍有再升息
可能，待能源價格與通膨壓力緩和、升息預期降溫後，利率
才有回落空間，但難降至3%以下。
◼ 歐洲企業資產負債表健康、獲利具韌性，緩衝高利率衝擊。
◼ 高利率限制歐股估值擴張，歐股維持中立，聚焦獲利成長與
政策支撐的產業：銀行受惠於目前利率環境與信貸需求，工

業、電氣化/能源安全及AI受惠戰略自主與資本支出提升。
Sep. 29, 2026
林亞瑱
歐洲總經政策與市場分析

能源價格大漲引發政策路徑重估為利率上揚主因
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026/9/15，中國信託商業銀行彙整，2026/9。
德國公債殖利率曲線上移，短率升幅大於長率
中東衝突發生以來德國公債殖利率曲線(中東地緣衝突前 vs. 目前)
近期全球公債殖利率大幅上揚 ，歐洲國家亦然，德國10年期公
債殖利率9/28升破3.6%，創2009年6月來新高，若從2月底中

東地緣衝突爆發以來起算 ，德國十年債利率累積彈升了近 100
個基點，30年期德債利率亦逼近4%(至9/28止)。
Q1：什麼因素推動今年歐洲公債殖利率走高？
(1)能源價格上漲推升通膨、市場重估歐央貨幣政策路徑，為驅
動本波利率上揚的主因：
2月底中東衝突爆發以來 ，德國10年期公債殖利率與油氣價格
走勢高度相關。此外，德國公債各期限殖利率全面上移 ，其中
短率升幅高於長率 ，反映本波利率上揚主 要受到能源價格上升
所帶動的通膨風險，以及市場對ECB政策路徑重新定價的影響。
此外，美國殖利率上升透過全球資本市場產生外溢效果 ，帶動
全球無風險利率中樞上移 ，亦對歐洲公債殖利率形成上行壓力 。

歐洲政府發債規模增與ECB縮表同步推升債券供給
■淨債券發行 ■ECB縮表 ◆對私人部門的淨供給
(2)歐洲公債供給增加、央行持續縮表，推升期限溢價：
德國擴大財政支出，歐洲政府融資需求及公債供給增加；同時 ，
ECB持續進行資產負債表正常化 ，逐步減少公債持有量 ，使新
增債券供給需由私人投資人吸收 ，推升投資人要求的期限溢價 。
除此之外，政府債務持有者結構轉變 ，個人與海外投資人等對
價格較為敏感的投資人占比增加，使債市更容易受到市場情緒
與金融條件變化的影響。
(3)財政疑慮使部分歐洲國家利差承受額外壓力：
歐洲部分國家面臨較高的政府債務壓力，加上政治局勢限制財
政調整空間，市場對債務可持續性的疑慮難消 。尤其在年度預
算協商期間，財政脆弱性較高的國家如法國 、英國，主權債務

風險重新定價容易反映在長端殖利率及與德債的利差擴大。

需求及投資之風險，並就投資結果自行負責。未經本公司許可，本資料及訊息不得逕行抄錄、翻印或另作派發。本行以上市場資訊與分析，不
涉及特定投資標的之建議。
Q2：面對公債殖利率上升，歐洲政府如何因應？
面對公債殖利率上升 ，歐洲政府調整發債結構 、增加短年期債
券發行，以控制高利率環境下的融資成本 ，預期此趨勢將延續
至2027年；惟縮短債務期限雖可降低當期融資成本，也將提高
中期再融資風險。
Q3：萬一歐洲主權債市場出現無序波動，歐洲有哪些
政策工具可以運用？
歐洲央行主要政策工具有二 ，分別為直接貨幣交易 (OMT)與傳
導保護工具(TPI)。
(1)直接貨幣交易(OMT)：2012年9月推出，源自歐債危機，可
在次級市場購買符合條件國家的1至3年期政府公債，且購買
規模事前不設上限 ，惟須搭配歐洲穩定機制 (ESM)的金融援
助計畫，並具嚴格的附帶條件，使用門檻較高，由主權國家

主動申請。
(2)傳導保護工具 (TPI)：2022年7月推出 ，可在次級市場購買
符合條件國家的 1至10年期政府部門債券 ，目的在於因應與
該國基本面不相稱的無序市場波動 ，避免金融市場分裂影響
貨幣政策傳導。啟動時將評估該國是否符合歐盟財政規範 、
債務是否具備可持續性等條件 ，由歐央評估後決定是否啟動。
不過，OMT與TPI均屬市場失序時的最後手段 ，並非用於壓低
正常的主權債殖利率或消除國家間的基本面利差 ，啟動有嚴格
條件，歐央不會輕易動用，目前此兩項工具均未實際動用過。
高利率下，歐洲政府調整發債、央行備有穩市工具

需求及投資之風險，並就投資結果自行負責。未經本公司許可，本資料及訊息不得逕行抄錄、翻印或另作派發。本行以上市場資訊與分析，不
涉及特定投資標的之建議。
Q4：歐洲政府債務的可持續性是否有立即風險？
主要歐洲國家除德國與西班牙外 ，IMF預估2026年政府債務佔
GDP比重均超過 100%。同時，近年地緣衝突頻率升高 ，戰略
自主與強化地區安全已成為歐洲核心目標 。為了讓成員國有擴
大國防支出的空間 ，歐盟允許成員國啟動 國家豁免條款，得以
在增加國防支出的同時 ，暫時不受歐盟財政規則約束 ，截至目
前已有18個歐盟會員國申請。國防支出提升，若未搭配增加財
政收入或其他支出調整，將使政府債務維持在較高路徑。
所幸，從利率與經濟成長率差距來看 ，目前利率水準尚未對歐
洲整體債務可持續性構成 立即威脅，但部分高負債國家的債務
壓力仍須留意。過去十年低利率環境的效益逐步消退 ，利率與

經濟成長率的差距 (r－g)較過去不利 ，加上期限溢價回升 ，使
高負債國家的債務動態面臨風險。
檢視歐元區四大經濟體 ，德國、西班牙政府債務的利率低於經
濟成長率，債務動態相對穩健；義大利政府債務 佔GDP比重最
高，所幸在政府控制支出下，自2024年以來維持初級財政盈餘，
有助控制債務比率進一步擴大。法國利率雖略低於經濟成長率 ，
但政府債務已接近 GDP的120%，在財政赤字偏高 、利息支出
持續增加且財政整頓進展有限 的情況下，未來兩年債務佔GDP
比率預期將持續上升，財政脆弱性相對較高。

Q5：近期法德利差顯著擴大，驅動因素與對法國股債
市的影響？
財政與政治不確定性升溫 ，推升法國主權風險溢價 ：法國步入
財政與政治不確定性升溫時期 ，不利的財政前景 ，加上4Q26
預算協商與2027年將舉行總統大選，政治不確定性升溫推升十
年期法德利差9月下旬突破100個基點，9/28升至112個基點、
創2012年6月以來新高。法國總統大選將於2027年4~5月舉行，
4/18舉行第一輪投票 ，若無候選人過半 ，5/2將舉行第二輪投
票。由於法國政治版圖高度分裂 、且極右派支持度領先 ，相較
於過往選舉周期，2027年法國選舉的不確定更高，預估將令市
場在2027年總統大選前維持謹慎情緒。
民調反映政治版圖分裂 ，增加財政政策的不確定性： 民調顯示，
極右派國民聯盟候選人瑪琳·勒龐在總統大選首輪的支持度領先，
超過三成，極左派法國不屈黨候選人梅朗雄 、中間偏右候選人
愛德華·菲利普民調落在15~18%左右。法國大選高機率將會進
入第二輪投票，取決於進入第二輪的候選人 ，目前民調顯示以

中間偏右候選人菲利普最有希望擊敗勒龐 ，若為極左派梅朗雄
進入第二輪，則勒龐勝選機率較高。

極端政治勢力候選人在本次大選勝選的可能性提高 ，為本已脆
弱的法國財政增添另一層不確定性 。與以往政治週期不同 ，法
國進入2027年大選週期時，財政迴旋餘地相當有限，加上法國
經濟成長弱於預期與利息成本上升，加大財政調整難度。
股債市影響：財政與政治變數將令法國資產風險溢價維持高位
(1)債市： 巴克萊資本 (2026/8)預估三種情境： (a)正向情境：
中東衝突告一段落 、秋季預算協商順利 、中間派候選人民調上
升，十年期法德利差有機會收斂至60~65個基點；(b)不利情境：
秋季預算協商引發市場雜音 、中東衝突進一步推高升息預期 ，
總統大選民調維持較高不確定性 ，法 德 利差 預 估 將 擴 大 至

95~100個基點；(c)極端情境：沒有中間派候選人進入第二輪
總統大選，法德利差可能突破100個基點。
9/28十年期法德利差已升至 112個基點，顯示市場已反映相當
程度的政治風險，再大幅惡化的空間應有限 。儘管如此，不確
定性短期難消，預估法德利差仍將維持在(b)情境高檔盤整。
(2)股市：法國股市估值相較歐股處於折價，且投資人對法國股
市的配置已明顯降低 ，有助緩和股市大幅修正的風險 。然而，
短線亦缺乏驅動股市行情的催化劑 ，法國經濟成長預測持續下
修、經濟動能落後歐元區 ，加上政治不確定性將影響投資人配
置意願，預估法國股市在2027年4月總統大選前波動仍大 ，金
融與內需導向型企業估值預估將承壓。

Q6：德債利率後續走勢？
貨幣政策正常化與結構因素 ，德債利率中樞上移： 低實質利率
環境已改變，根據歐洲央行執委Schnabel(2025/2)分析，過去
壓低實質利率的部分結構性因素正 在逆轉。一方面，地緣政治
碎片化、氣候變遷及勞動力短缺 ，使中長期通膨上行風險增加；
另一方面，全球由「儲蓄過剩」逐步轉向「債券供給過剩 」，
政府財政赤字擴大與央行資產負債表正常化 ，使安全資產供給
增加、稀缺性下降 ，進而推升實質利率 。疫情後實質利率上升
究竟是暫時現象 ，抑或代表新的長期均衡目前雖難預測 ，但從
全球儲蓄與投資平衡 的變化，地緣政治與氣候變遷等結構挑戰 ，
實質利率上升似乎是較有可能出現的情況。
利率短期維持高檔 ，待通膨與升息預期緩和才有回落空間： 能
源價格衝擊與通膨傳導將是牽動後續利率的主要因素 。歐央
2026年已升息二碼，存款機制利率上調至2.5%的中性區間上緣。

中東衝突持續下 ，歐央上調2027~2028年通膨預期 ，預計中期
內通膨率和核心通膨率將高於2%政策目標。目前天然氣期貨價
格已偏離歐央基準情境 、往不利情境靠攏 ，成品油價格因裂解
利差跳升而居高不下 ，因此，除非中東局勢迅速平息 ，否則預
估12月將再升一碼至 2.75%。9/28利率期貨反映 市場預期2027
年9月前將再升息超過3碼。不過，高能源價格若 延續較久，將
抑制經濟成長 ，殖利率上升也將發揮緊縮效果 、削弱通膨傳導 ，
我們預期歐央持續升息的必要性低於目前市場定價 。後續利率
上行空間將取決於能源價格與通膨是否進一步超出預期 ，待通
膨與升息預期緩和 ，利率將有回落空間 。惟考量結構性因素推
升，德國10年期公債殖利率預期將維持在3%以上；若要回落至
3%以下，可能需要看到歐央再次調降政策利率 ，預估4Q27才
有機會降息。

Q7：高利率對歐洲企業的影響？
首先，歐洲企業資產負債表整體維持健康 ，歐洲企業近年持續
去槓桿，淨債務/EBITDA比率維持相對低檔 ，整體財務槓桿不
高。而且，企業債務期限普遍較長 ，平均到期期限約五年 ，高
利率對企業利息負擔的傳導較為緩慢 ，因此，即使公債殖利率

上升，企業債務成本的重新定價將逐步反映 ，降低企業對利率
上升的敏感度。
另一方面 ，企業信貸需求仍具韌性 ，8月歐元區非金融企業貸
款年增4.2%，顯示截至目前企業融資活動並未因利率走高而明
顯降溫。此外，儘管歐洲公債殖利率大幅走高 ，歐洲投資級債
及非投資級債信用利差並未明顯擴大 ，顯示企業信用基本面 並
未出現明顯惡化。
整體而言，企業資產負債表維持健康 ，融資需求仍具韌性 ，信
用風險未明顯升高，高利率目前對企業的直接衝擊有限。
資料來源：Bloomberg，2005年以來至2026/9/23，中國信託商業銀行彙整，2026/9。

Q8：歐股展望？
2026年以來至9/28止，歐洲道瓊50指數上漲9%，過去一個月
出現回檔 ，能源價格再度上漲 、TTF天然氣期貨價格飆升至三
年多高點，央行立場偏鷹、公債殖利率大幅走高 ，加上歐洲政
治雜音增加，均對盤勢形成壓力 。然而，歐股自高點以來波段
回檔不到5%、幅度可控，企業獲利維持韌性扮演重要緩衝。
STOXX歐洲600企業2026年上半年每股盈餘較去年同期成長

14%，創近3年來最快增速 。雖然獲利成長主要由商品類股貢
獻，但商品以外類股上半年每股盈餘年增率亦上修至 7%。高
盛證券(2026/8)預估，2026年下半年歐洲企業獲利年增率將進
一步加速至19%，全年獲利預估成長15%。
短線歐股盤勢將受到油價與利率牽動 ，過去一個月德國十年期
公債殖利率跳升 36個基點 ，壓抑歐股評價 ，預估本益比降至
14.7倍(9/28)。目前美伊仍透過斡旋進行接觸 ，惟尚未看到達
成和解的明確路徑 ，在能源價格維持高檔 、通膨上行風險仍存
之下，歐央可能進一步升息，此外，2027年歐洲多國進入選舉
周期，政治不確定性將干擾市場情緒 ，令本益比擴張空間受限。
2027年企業獲利增速預估將由 2026年的高雙位數放緩至中個
位數，但仍維持穩定成長 ，將為股市提供基本面支撐 ，惟股市
動能預期將較為溫和。
Q9：高利率環境下，歐股布局聚焦哪些方向？
利率維持高檔、指數評價擴張空間有限下 ，歐股展望維持中立 ，
相對看好具獲利成長或政策支撐的產業 。銀行股受惠於目前利

率環境及企業信貸需求回升；工業 /國防、電氣化/能源安全及
AI等概念股則受惠於政策支持及相關投資需求增加。

## Document 534: ステーブルコインは「国債の買い手」になるのか ～デジタルドルが米国債を支える構図は、日本でも生まれるのか～

Source PDF: all report pdf/ステーブルコインは「国債の買い手」になるのか ～デジタルドルが米国債を支える構図は、日本でも生まれるのか～.pdf
Report source: DLRI
Date: 2026-11-02
Pages: 6
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Research Report

2026 年 11 月 2 日

1/6 
ステーブルコインは「国債の買い手」になるのか 
～デジタルドルが米国債を支える構図は、日本でも生まれるのか～ 
 資産運用調査部 主席研究員  柏村 祐

（要旨） 
○ 米国の巨額の財政赤字や伝統的な買い手の保有縮小により、米国債の「需給懸念」と金利高
止まりが意識される中、暗号資産市場から生まれた「ステーブルコイン」の発行体が新たな
買い手として浮上している。 
○ ステーブルコイン発行体による米国債保有は急増しており、中国の保有減少を一部補う規模
に達している。また、IMF の実証分析でも、ステーブルコインへの需要増加が短期米国債利
回りに低下圧力を及ぼすことが示されている。 
○ 一方、法定通貨担保型ステーブルコインの99.4％は米ドル連動であり、円建ての市場規模は
極めて小さい。当面、円建てステーブルコインが日本国債の需給を支える存在になることは
期待しにくい。 
○ 個人投資家は、米金利を占う上でステーブルコイン市場の動向を新たな金融市場の指標とし
て注視しつつ、国内資産については従来型の金利上昇（債券安）リスクへの備えを継続する
ことが対応策の一つとなる。

1. 米国債の「需給懸念」と金利高止まり～新たな買い手は誰か～ 
米国の金利動向は、世界の金融市場や個人投資家のポートフォリオに多大な影響を及ぼす。足
元では、インフレの高止まりや米連邦準備制度理事会（FRB）の金融政策動向に加え、米国債の
「需給懸念」が長期金利の上昇（債券価格の下落）を招く構造的な要因の一つとして意識されて
いる。米政府の財政赤字が拡大を続ける一方で、米国債の主要な海外保有国の一つである中国
は、外貨準備の多様化などを背景に米国債の保有を減らしつつある。こうした伝統的な買い手の
一部が保有を減らす中、 「一体誰が今後の米国債を買い支えるのか」という問いは、米国株のバリ
ュエーションやドル円相場の行方を左右する論点である。 
こうした中、米国債の新たな買い手として急速に存在感を高めているのが、暗号資産市場から
生まれた「ステーブルコイン」である。法定通貨に価値を連動させる代表的なステーブルコイン
は、その価値を裏付けるために巨額の準備資産を必要とする。 
ステーブルコインは米国債の有力な買い手となり、米国債の利回り上昇を抑える要因になり得

### Page 2

2/6 
るのか。また、デジタルドルが米国債を支えるこの構図は、日本国債と円建てステーブルコイン
の間でも起こり得るのだろうか。本稿では、最近の実証研究を手がかりにこの資金の流れを検証
し、個人投資家にとっての論点を整理する。

2. ステーブルコインは国債の新たな買い手となるか 
(1) 米国債の新たな買い手は誰か 
近年、暗号資産市場の拡大とともに、法定通貨（主に米ドル）に価値を連動させる「ステーブ
ルコイン」の発行残高が拡大している。その発行体は、発行したコインの価値を裏付けるため、
準備資産として安全かつ流動性の高い資産を保有する必要がある。その代表格が短期米国債（米
国財務省短期証券、T-bills）である。 
サンフランシスコ連銀が2026年 9 月に公表したレポートによれば、ステーブルコイン発行体に
よる米国債の保有が急増しており、米国債市場における新たな買い手として浮上している。過去
20年間、米国債の外国人保有比率は低下傾向にあり、特に中国などの外国政府による保有減少が
目立っていた。しかし、ステーブルコイン発行体は過去5年間で米国債関連資産の保有額を約
2,000 億ドル増加させており、これは同時期の中国による米国債保有減少額の4 割超に相当する。
ただし、中国の保有減少は主に長期国債であるのに対し、ステーブルコイン発行体の保有増加は
主に短期米国債であり、満期構成には違いがある。 
資料 1は、中国とステーブルコイン発行体（時価総額上位2銘柄であるUSDTおよびUSDCの発行
体（それぞれTetherおよびCircle））の米国債保有額の推移を示している。足元のトレンドが継続
した場合、ステーブルコイン発行体による短期米国債の需要は、2030年末までに約 4,000 億ドル
へとほぼ倍増するとの試算も示されている。伝統的な買い手が後退するなか、短期ゾーンに限っ
ていえば、ステーブルコイン発行体はすでに主要な買い手の一角を占めつつある。

### Page 3

3/6

資料 1 ステーブルコイン発行体の米国債保有額は急増し、中国の保有減少を一部補う 
（注）ステーブルコイン発行体は時価総額上位 2 社（Tether（USDT）および Circle（USDC））を指す。保有額には米国財務省短期 
証券（T-bills）および現先取引（レポ）を含む。 
（出所）Federal Reserve Bank of San Francisco, “Stablecoin Issuers’ Growing Appetite for Treasury Securities”をもとに第一ラ 
イフ資産運用経済研究所作成

(2) ステーブルコインは国債金利を押し下げるのか 
ステーブルコインによる米国債需要の増加は、実際の金利形成にどのような影響を及ぼすのだ
ろうか。国際通貨基金（IMF）が 2026年3 月に発表したワーキングペーパーは、ステーブルコイ
ンの需要増加が金融市場に与える影響を実証的に分析している。同ペーパーでは、マクロ経済全
体の動き（好景気や株高など）による影響をできるだけ切り分けるため、ステーブルコイン特有
のニュース（規制動向や技術提携など）を手掛かりに、需要の変動を識別する手法（ナラティブ
識別）を用いている。 
資料 2は、この手法を用いて「ステーブルコインの時価総額をピーク時に1％増加させる需要シ
ョック」が生じた際、1カ月物米国債利回りが時間の経過とともにどう反応するか（インパルス応
答）を推計したものである。これを見ると、ショック発生後に利回りは持続的に低下し、約24 週
後に最大で約1.9ベーシスポイント（bp）の押し下げ効果が生じると推計されている。押し下げ
幅そのものは小さいが、ステーブルコインへの需要が国債市場の需給を通じて、短期金利に波及
する経路が実証的に示された意義は小さくない。暗号資産市場の動きが、もはや同市場内の出来

### Page 4

4/6 
事にとどまらなくなっていることを示している。

資料 2 ステーブルコインへの需要増加は短期米国債利回りの低下要因となる 
（注）ステーブルコイン（USDT および USDC）の合計時価総額をピーク時に 1％増加させる需要ショックに対する、1 カ月物米国債 
利回りの推定反応（インパルス応答）。「ナラティブ識別」とは、ニュースなどの定性的な情報をもとに、他の経済要因の影響
を受けにくい出来事を選び、特定のショックを識別する分析手法。青色の実線は推定値、濃い青色と薄い水色の網掛け部分
はそれぞれ 68％および 90％の信頼区間を示す。 
（出所）IMF, “Stablecoin Shocks” (WP/2026/044)をもとに第一ライフ資産運用経済研究所作成

(3) 円建てステーブルコインは日本国債の買い手になるか 
米国市場で起きている「デジタルドル（米ドル建てステーブルコイン）が米国債を支える」と
いう構図は、日本でも起きるのだろうか。日本においても、法整備が進み円建てステーブルコイ
ンの発行・流通が本格化すれば、その準備資産として日本国債（特に短期国債）への新たな需要
を生み出す余地はある。 
しかし、現時点では米ドル建てと円建ての市場規模には圧倒的な差が存在する。国際決済銀行
（BIS）が2026年 6 月に公表した「Annual Economic Report 2026」によれば、法定通貨担保型ス
テーブルコインの99.4％が米ドルに連動している。資料3 は、米ドル建て以外のステーブルコイ
ンの時価総額を米ドル建てに対する比率で示したものだが、ユーロ建てやブラジルレアル建てな
どと並び、円建て（JPY）の規模は米ドル建ての0.01％台に過ぎず、極めて小さい水準にとどまっ
ている。 
円建てステーブルコインが日本国債の有力な買い手として市場に影響を与えるようになるに
は、決済や価値保存の手段として円建てステーブルコインのユースケースがグローバルに拡大
し、時価総額が飛躍的に成長することが前提となる。円建ての規模が米ドル建ての1万分の1 程
度にとどまる現状を踏まえれば、当面、円建てステーブルコインが日本国債の需給を左右する局

### Page 5

5/6 
面は想定しにくい。日本の投資家にとっての論点は、円建てではなく、米国市場ですでに起きて
いる変化の側にある。

資料 3 米ドル建て以外のステーブルコインの市場規模は依然として極めて小さい 
（注）米ドル建てステーブルコインの時価総額に対する各通貨建てステーブルコインの時価総額の比率（％）。2026 年 5 月 29 日 
時点。法定通貨担保型ステーブルコインのみを対象とする。 
（出所）BIS, “Annual Economic Report 2026”をもとに第一ライフ資産運用経済研究所作成

3. 個人投資家へのインプリケーション～「暗号資産×マクロ」の新視点～ 
前節で見てきたように、ステーブルコインの発行体は米国債市場で存在感を高めており、その
需要拡大が短期金利に低下圧力を及ぼすことも実証研究によって示されている。一方で、円建て
ステーブルコインの市場規模は極めて小さく、日本国債の需給に影響を及ぼす存在になるには、
なお市場の大幅な拡大を要する。 
個人投資家にとっての含意は2 点ある。 
第一に、米国の金利動向を占う上で、「ステーブルコイン市場の動向」を新たな金融市場の指標
として注視すべきであるということだ。これまで、暗号資産は伝統的な株式や債券とは異なる投
機的な投資対象と見なされがちだった。しかし、ステーブルコインを介して暗号資産市場の資金
が国債市場に流れ込み、金利形成に影響を与え始めている。暗号資産を保有していない投資家で
あっても、ステーブルコインの時価総額の伸びや、米国で成立したGENIUS法の 2027年 1月の施
行や関連規制の動向が、自身の保有する米国株や米国債券ファンドの価格に影響を及ぼす可能性
がある点に留意が必要だ。 
第二に、日本国内の金利上昇リスクに対する警戒を怠らないことである。米国ではステーブル
コインという「新たな買い手」が国債需給を下支えする構造が生まれつつあるが、日本では当面
同様の需給下支え効果は期待しにくい。日本銀行（日銀）が国債買入れの減額を進め、長年市場
を支えてきた最大の買い手が後退する中、日本国債についても、需給の緩みを通じた金利上昇圧
力には注意が必要である。

### Page 6

本資料は情報提供を目的として作成されたものであり、投資勧誘を目的としたものではありません。作成時点で、第一ライフ資産運用
経済研究所が信ずるに足ると判断した情報に基づき作成していますが、その正確性、完全性に対する責任は負いません。見通しは予告
なく変更されることがあります。また、記載された内容は、第一生命保険ないしはその関連会社の投資方針と常に整合的であるとは限
りません。 
6/6 
個人投資家にとっては、ステーブルコインの拡大による米国短期国債の利回り低下を、一つの
市場シナリオとして念頭に置きつつも、国内資産については、保有する債券のデュレーション
（金利変動に対する価格の感応度）を短めに保つなど、従来型の金利上昇（債券安）への備えを
継続することが、対応策の一つとなる。

以 上
